\textbf{Q.106} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS1\_CS\_Q18}}

Consider the following C program:
\begin{lstlisting}[language=C]
#include <stdio.h>
int main() {
    int a = 6;
    int b = 0;
    while(a < 10) {
        a = a / 12 + 1;
        a += b;
    }
    printf("%d", a);
    return 0;
}
\end{lstlisting}
Which one of the following statements is CORRECT?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} The program prints 9 as output \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} The program prints 10 as output \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} The program gets stuck in an infinite loop \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} The program prints 6 as output
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large C} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Programming and Data Structures \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Programming in C}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Initially $a = 6, b = 0$. In the loop, integer division $a/12 = 6/12 = 0$, so $a = 0 + 1 = 1$, and $a += b \implies a = 1$. In subsequent iterations, $a=1 < 10$, $1/12 + 1 = 1$, so $a$ remains 1 in every iteration. The loop condition $a < 10$ remains true indefinitely, causing an infinite loop.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent

    sidebyside align=top,

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.107} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS1\_CS\_Q19}}

Consider the following C program:
\begin{lstlisting}[language=C]
#include <stdio.h>
void fX();
int main() {
    fX();
    return 0;
}
void fX() {
    char a;
    if((a = getchar()) != '\n')
        fX();
    if(a != '\n')
        putchar(a);
}
\end{lstlisting}
Assume that the input to the program from the command line is `1234` followed by a newline character. Which one of the following statements is CORRECT?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} The program will not terminate \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} The program will terminate with no output \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} The program will terminate with 4321 as output \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} The program will terminate with 1234 as output
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large C} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Programming and Data Structures \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Recursion}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small The function `fX()` recursively reads each character before printing. As each character (`'1'`, `'2'`, `'3'`, `'4'`) is read, it is saved on the call stack before `fX()` recurses. When `\n` is reached, recursion terminates and the characters are printed during stack unwinding in reverse (LIFO) order as `4321`.}

\end{tcolorbox}

\textbf{Q.108} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS2\_CS\_Q13}}

Consider the following C program. Assume parameters to a function are evaluated from right to left.
\begin{verbatim}
#include <stdio.h>
int g(int p) { printf("%d", p); return p; }
int h(int q) { printf("%d", q); return q; }
void f(int x, int y) {
    g(x);
    h(y);
}
int main() {
    f(g(10), h(20));
}
\end{verbatim}
Which one of the following is the output of the program?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} 20101020 & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} 10202010 \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} 20102010 & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} 10201020
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Programming and Data Structures \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Programming in C}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small In `main()`, `f(g(10), h(20))` evaluates parameters from right to left: `h(20)` executes first, printing `20` and returning 20 ($y = 20$), then `g(10)` executes, printing `10` and returning 10 ($x = 10$). Inside `f`, `g(x)` executes printing `10`, followed by `h(y)` printing `20`. The complete printed sequence is `20101020`.}

\end{tcolorbox}

\textbf{Q.109} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS2\_CS\_Q33}}

Consider the following C function definition:
\begin{verbatim}
int fX(char *a){
    char *b = a;
    while(*b)
        b++;
    return b - a;
}
\end{verbatim}
Which of the following statements is/are TRUE?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} The function call fX("abcd") will always return a value \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} Assuming a character array c is declared as char c[] = "abcd" in main(), the function call fX(c) will always return a value \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} The code of the function will not compile \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} Assuming a character pointer c is declared as char *c = "abcd" in main(), the function call fX(c) will always return a value
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A;B;D} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Programming and Data Structures \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Programming in C}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small The function `fX` calculates the string length by advancing pointer `b` until the null terminator `\0` is reached, then returning pointer difference `b - a`. String literals in C are null-terminated, so calls with string literals or character arrays (`fX("abcd")`, `fX(c)`) correctly compute 4 and return (A, B, and D are true).}

\end{tcolorbox}

\textbf{Q.110} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS1\_CS\_Q26}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Which of the following statement(s) is/are TRUE for any binary search tree (BST) having $n$ distinct integers?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} The maximum length of a path from the root node to any other node is $(n-1)$. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} An inorder traversal will always produce a sorted sequence of elements. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} Finding an element takes $O(\log_2 n)$ time in the worst case. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} Every BST is also a Min-Heap.
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A;B} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Programming and Data Structures \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Trees, binary search trees, binary heaps}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small In a degenerate (skewed) binary search tree, the path length from root to the farthest leaf is $n - 1$ edges (A is true). Inorder traversal of any valid BST visits nodes in strictly increasing order (B is true). Worst-case search in an unbalanced BST takes $O(n)$ time, and BST property differs fundamentally from heap order.}

\end{tcolorbox}

\textbf{Q.111} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[NAT $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS1\_CS\_Q34}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider the following C program:
\par\medskip
\begin{verbatim}
#include <stdio.h>
void foo(int *p, int x){
    *p = x;
}
int main(){
    int *z;
    int a = 20, b = 25;
    z = &a;
    foo(z, b);
    printf("%d", a);
    return 0;
}
\end{verbatim}
The output of the given C program is \_\_\_\_\_\_\. (Answer in integer)

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent\textbf{Your Answer:} \fbox{\rule{0pt}{1.3ex}\hspace{3cm}}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large 25 to 25} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Programming and Data Structures \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Programming in C}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small In `main()`, pointer `z` is assigned the memory address of variable `a` (`\&a`). Function `foo(z, b)` dereferences `p` (which points to `a`) and assigns `x = b = 25` to `*p`. Thus, `a` is updated to 25, which `printf` prints.}

\end{tcolorbox}

\textbf{Q.112} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[NAT $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS1\_CS\_Q35}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

The height of any rooted tree is defined as the maximum number of edges in the path from the root node to any leaf node. Suppose a Min-Heap $T$ stores 32 keys. The height of $T$ is \_\_\_\_\_\_\. (Answer in integer)

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent\textbf{Your Answer:} \fbox{\rule{0pt}{1.3ex}\hspace{3cm}}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large 5 to 5} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Programming and Data Structures \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Trees, binary search trees, binary heaps}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small A binary heap storing $n$ keys is an almost complete binary tree whose height in terms of edges is $\lfloor \log_2 n \rfloor$. For $n = 32$, the height is $\lfloor \log_2 32 \rfloor = 5$.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

\textbf{Q.113} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS2\_CS\_Q13}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider a binary tree $T$ in which every node has either zero or two children. Let $n > 0$ be the number of nodes in $T$. Which ONE of the following is the number of nodes in $T$ that have exactly two children?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} $\frac{n-2}{2}$ & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} $\frac{n-1}{2}$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} $\frac{n}{2}$ & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} $\frac{n+1}{2}$
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Programming and Data Structures \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Trees and strictly binary trees}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small In any proper (strictly) binary tree with $L$ leaves and $I$ internal nodes (nodes with 2 children), $L = I + 1$. Since $n = L + I = 2I + 1$, the number of internal nodes is $I = \frac{n-1}{2}$.}

\end{tcolorbox}

\textbf{Q.114} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS2\_CS\_Q19}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider the following C program:
\begin{lstlisting}[language=C, basicstyle=\footnotesize\ttfamily, frame=single, rulecolor=\color{black!30}, tabsize=2, breaklines=true]
#include <stdio.h>
void stringcopy(char *, char *);
int main() {
    char a[30] = "@#Hello World!";
    stringcopy(a, a + 2);
    printf("%s\n", a);
    return 0;
}
void stringcopy(char *s, char *t) {
    while(*t)
        *s++ = *t++;
}
\end{lstlisting}
Which ONE of the following will be the output of the program?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} @\#Hello World! & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} Hello World! \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} ello World! & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} Hello World!d!
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large D} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Programming and Data Structures \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Programming in C: pointers and strings}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small `stringcopy(a, a + 2)` copies string `"Hello World!"` into the start of array `a`, overwriting the first 12 characters. The copy stops when `*t` hits null terminator without copying the null terminator over `d!`, leaving trailing `d!\0`, resulting in `"Hello World!d!"`.}

\end{tcolorbox}

\textbf{Q.115} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[NAT $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS2\_CS\_Q33}}

Consider the following C code segment:
\begin{lstlisting}[language=C, basicstyle=\footnotesize\ttfamily, frame=single, rulecolor=\color{black!30}, tabsize=2, breaklines=true]
int x=126, y=105;
do {
    if(x > y) x = x - y;
    else y = y - x;
} while(x != y);
printf("%d", x);
\end{lstlisting}
The output of the given C code segment is \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_. (Answer in integer)

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent\textbf{Your Answer:} \fbox{\rule{0pt}{1.3ex}\hspace{3cm}}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large 21 to 21} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Programming and Data Structures \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Programming in C: loops and Euclidean GCD}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small The do-while loop implements the subtraction-based Euclidean algorithm for greatest common divisor: $\gcd(x, y) = \gcd(126, 105) = 21$.}

\end{tcolorbox}

Suppose the values 10, $-4$, 15, 30, 20, 5, 60, 19 are inserted in that order into an initially empty binary search tree. Let $T$ be the resulting binary search tree. The number of edges in the path from the node containing 19 to the root node of $T$ is \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_. (Answer in integer)

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent\textbf{Your Answer:} \fbox{\rule{0pt}{1.3ex}\hspace{3cm}}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large 4 to 4} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Programming and Data Structures \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Trees: binary search trees}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Insert keys into BST: 10 is root $\to$ 15 goes right $\to$ 30 goes right $\to$ 20 goes left $\to$ 19 goes left of 20. The path from root is $10 \to 15 \to 30 \to 20 \to 19$, which contains 4 edges.}

\end{tcolorbox}

\textbf{Q.117} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2022 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2022\_CS\_CS\_Q11}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Which one of the following statements is TRUE for all positive functions $f(n)$?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} $f(n^2) = \Theta((f(n))^2)$, when $f(n)$ is a polynomial \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} $f(n^2) = o((f(n))^2)$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} $f(n^2) = \mathcal{O}((f(n))^2)$, when $f(n)$ is an exponential function \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} $f(n^2) = \Omega((f(n))^2)$
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Algorithms \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Asymptotic worst case time and space complexity}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small If $f(n)$ is a polynomial of degree $k$ ($f(n) = \Theta(n^k)$), then $f(n^2) = \Theta((n^2)^k) = \Theta(n^{2k})$, and $(f(n))^2 = (\Theta(n^k))^2 = \Theta(n^{2k})$. Since both functions grow at identical polynomial rates, $f(n^2) = \Theta((f(n))^2)$ holds.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.118} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2022 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2022\_CS\_CS\_Q16}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Suppose we are given $n$ keys, $m$ hash table slots, and two simple uniform hash functions $h_1$ and $h_2$. Further suppose our hashing scheme uses $h_1$ for the odd keys and $h_2$ for the even keys. What is the expected number of keys in a slot?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} $m/n$ & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} $n/m$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} $2n/m$ & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} $n / (2m)$
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Algorithms \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Searching, sorting, hashing}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Let $n_1$ be the number of odd keys and $n_2$ be the number of even keys ($n_1 + n_2 = n$). Under simple uniform hashing, each key independently hashes to any slot with probability $1/m$. By linearity of expectation, the expected number of keys in any slot is $\frac{n_1}{m} + \frac{n_2}{m} = \frac{n}{m}$.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.119} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2023 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2023\_CS\_CS\_Q20}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

An algorithm has to store several keys generated by an adversary in a hash table. The adversary is malicious who tries to maximize the number of collisions. Let $k$ be the number of keys, $m$ be the number of slots in the hash table, and $k > m$.
Which one of the following is the best hashing strategy to counteract the adversary?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} Division method, i.e., use the hash function $h(k) = k \bmod m$. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} Multiplication method, i.e., use the hash function $h(k) = \lfloor m(kA - \lfloor kA \rfloor)\rfloor$, where $A$ is a carefully chosen constant. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} Universal hashing method. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} If $k$ is a prime number, use Division method. Otherwise, use Multiplication method.
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large C} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Algorithms \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Searching, sorting, hashing}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small In universal hashing, the hash function is chosen uniformly at random from a carefully designed universal collection of hash functions independently of the keys. Because the adversary cannot predict which function will be selected at runtime, any fixed choice of malicious keys cannot guarantee worst-case collision behavior.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

]
\textbf{Q.120} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2023 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2023\_CS\_CS\_Q29}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Let $f$ and $g$ be functions of natural numbers given by $f(n) = n$ and $g(n) = n^2$.
Which of the following statements is/are TRUE?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} $f \in \mathcal{O}(g)$ & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} $f \in \Omega(g)$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} $f \in o(g)$ & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} $f \in \Theta(g)$
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A, C} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Algorithms \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Asymptotic worst case time and space complexity}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small For $f(n) = n$ and $g(n) = n^2$, we evaluate $\lim_{n \to \infty} \frac{f(n)}{g(n)} = \lim_{n \to \infty} \frac{1}{n} = 0$. By definition, if the limit is 0, $f(n) \in o(g(n))$, which strictly implies $f(n) \in \mathcal{O}(g(n))$. Hence, both A and C are TRUE.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.121} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS1\_CS\_Q17}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Given an integer array of size $N$, we want to check if the array is sorted (in either ascending or descending order). An algorithm solves this problem by making a single pass through the array and comparing each element of the array only with its adjacent elements. The worst-case time complexity of this algorithm is

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} both $\mathcal{O}(N)$ and $\Omega(N)$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} $\mathcal{O}(N)$ but not $\Omega(N)$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} $\Omega(N)$ but not $\mathcal{O}(N)$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} neither $\mathcal{O}(N)$ nor $\Omega(N)$
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Algorithms \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Asymptotic worst case time and space complexity}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small In the worst case (such as when the array is sorted or the disorder occurs at the very end), a single-pass algorithm that compares adjacent elements must traverse the entire array, performing $N-1$ comparisons. The worst-case runtime is asymptotically tight at $\Theta(N)$, meaning it is both $\mathcal{O}(N)$ and $\Omega(N)$.}

\end{tcolorbox}

\textbf{Q.122} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[NAT $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS2\_CS\_Q35}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Let $A$ be an array containing integer values. The distance of $A$ is defined as the minimum number of elements in $A$ that must be replaced with another integer so that the resulting array is sorted in non-decreasing order. The distance of the array $[2, 5, 3, 1, 4, 2, 6]$ is \_\_\_\_\_\_\. (Answer in integer)

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent\textbf{Your Answer:} \fbox{\rule{0pt}{1.3ex}\hspace{3cm}}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large 3 to 3} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Algorithms \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Searching, sorting, hashing}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small To minimize replacements to make array $A$ sorted in non-decreasing order, keep the longest non-decreasing subsequence (LNDS). For $A = [2, 5, 3, 1, 4, 2, 6]$, a longest non-decreasing subsequence is $[2, 3, 4, 6]$ of length 4. The minimum elements to replace is $n - \text{length(LNDS)} = 7 - 4 = 3$.}

\end{tcolorbox}

\textbf{Q.123} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS1\_CS\_Q18}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Let $G$ be any undirected graph with positive edge weights, and $T$ be a minimum spanning tree of $G$. For any two vertices, $u$ and $v$, let $d_1(u, v)$ and $d_2(u, v)$ be the shortest distances between $u$ and $v$ in $G$ and $T$, respectively. Which ONE of the options is CORRECT for all possible $G, T, u$ and $v$?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} $d_1(u, v) = d_2(u, v)$ & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} $d_1(u, v) \le d_2(u, v)$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} $d_1(u, v) \ge d_2(u, v)$ & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} $d_1(u, v) \ne d_2(u, v)$
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Algorithms \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Graph traversals, minimum spanning trees, shortest paths}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Since the minimum spanning tree $T$ is a subgraph of $G$ ($E(T) \subseteq E(G)$), every path between $u$ and $v$ in $T$ is also a valid path in $G$. Thus, the shortest path distance in $G$ cannot exceed that in $T$, guaranteeing $d_1(u, v) \le d_2(u, v)$.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[
    blanker,
    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.124} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS1\_CS\_Q20}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider the following recurrence relation:
\[ T(n) = 2T(n-1) + n2^n \quad \text{for } n > 0, \; T(0) = 1. \]
Which ONE of the following options is CORRECT?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} $T(n) = \Theta(n^2 2^n)$ & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} $T(n) = \Theta(n 2^n)$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} $T(n) = \Theta((\log n)^2 2^n)$ & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} $T(n) = \Theta(4^n)$
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Algorithms \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Asymptotic worst case time and space complexity}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Dividing both sides by $2^n$ yields $\frac{T(n)}{2^n} = \frac{T(n-1)}{2^{n-1}} + n$. Letting $S(n) = \frac{T(n)}{2^n}$, we have $S(n) = S(n-1) + n = S(0) + \sum_{i=1}^n i = 1 + \frac{n(n+1)}{2} = \Theta(n^2)$. Multiplying back gives $T(n) = \Theta(n^2 2^n)$.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[
    blanker,
    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,
    middle=16pt,
    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.125} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[NAT $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS1\_CS\_Q33}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

The pseudocode of a function fun() is given below:
\begin{verbatim}
fun(int A[0,...,n-1]){
    for i=0 to n-2
        for j=0 to n-i-2
            if (A[j] > A[j+1])
                then swap A[j] and A[j+1]
}
\end{verbatim}
Let $A[0, \ldots, 29]$ be an array storing 30 distinct integers in descending order. The number of swap operations that will be performed, if the function fun() is called with $A[0, \ldots, 29]$ as argument, is \_\_\_\_\_\_\. (Answer in integer)

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent\textbf{Your Answer:} \fbox{\rule{0pt}{1.3ex}\hspace{3cm}}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large 435 to 435} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Algorithms \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Searching, sorting, hashing}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small The function implements standard Bubble Sort. When sorting an array of $n = 30$ elements in strictly reverse order, every comparison triggers a swap operation. The total number of swaps is $\sum_{i=0}^{n-2} (n - 1 - i) = \frac{n(n-1)}{2} = \frac{30 \times 29}{2} = 435$.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.126} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS2\_CS\_Q20}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider an unordered list of $N$ distinct integers. What is the minimum number of element comparisons required to find an integer in the list that is NOT the largest in the list?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} 1 & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} $N - 1$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} $N$ & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} $2N - 1$
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Algorithms \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Searching and comparison complexity}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small To find any element that is not the maximum, compare any two distinct elements in 1 comparison; the smaller of the two is guaranteed not to be the maximum.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --

\textbf{Q.127} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS2\_CS\_Q29}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Which of the following statements regarding Breadth First Search (BFS) and Depth First Search (DFS) on an undirected simple graph $G$ is/are TRUE?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} A DFS tree of $G$ is a Shortest Path tree of $G$. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} Every non-tree edge of $G$ with respect to a DFS tree is a forward/back edge. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} If $(u, v)$ is a non-tree edge of $G$ with respect to a BFS tree, then the distances from the source vertex $s$ to $u$ and $v$ in the BFS tree are within $\pm 1$ of each other. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} Both BFS and DFS can be used to find the connected components of $G$.
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B;C;D} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Algorithms \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Graph traversals: BFS and DFS}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small In an undirected graph, non-tree DFS edges can only be back edges (B is true). In a BFS tree, cross edges can only exist between the same or adjacent levels, so distances differ by at most $\pm 1$ (C is true). Both can compute connected components (D is true).}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

\textbf{Q.128} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2018 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2018\_CS\_GA\_Q06\_v2}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Let $N$ be an NFA with $n$ states. Let $k$ be the number of states of a minimal DFA which is equivalent to $N$. Which one of the following is necessarily true?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} $k \ge 2^n$ & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} $k \ge n$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} $k \le n^2$ & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} $k \le 2^n$
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large D} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Theory of Computation \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Regular expressions and finite automata}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small By the subset construction algorithm, converting an $n$-state NFA to a DFA produces states that are subsets of the NFA states. Thus, the power set has $2^n$ possible states, ensuring that any equivalent minimal DFA has $k \le 2^n$ states.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);

]
\textbf{Q.129} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2018 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2018\_CS\_GA\_Q07\_v2}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

The set of all recursively enumerable languages is

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} closed under complementation. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} closed under intersection. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} a subset of the set of all context-sensitive languages. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} an uncountable set.
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Theory of Computation \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Regular and context-free languages, pumping lemma}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small The set of recursively enumerable (RE) languages is closed under union, intersection, concatenation, and Kleene star, but not closed under complementation or set difference.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[

\textbf{Q.130} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2022 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2022\_CS\_CS\_Q12}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Which one of the following regular expressions correctly represents the language of the finite automaton given below?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} $ab^*ba^* + ba^*ab^*$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} $(ab^*b + ba^*a)^*(a + b)$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} $(ab^*b + ba^*a)^*(a^*b + b^*a)$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} $(ba^*a + ab^*b)^*(ab^* + ba^*)$
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large D} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Theory of Computation \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Regular expressions and finite automata}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small From the start state, loops on intermediate states allow repeating patterns of $ba^*a$ and $ab^*b$ before moving to an accepting state via either $ab^*$ or $ba^*$. Factoring loop paths and exit transitions yields the regular expression $(ba^*a + ab^*b)^*(ab^* + ba^*)$.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[

    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,

    }
]
\textbf{Q.131} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2022 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2022\_CS\_CS\_Q23}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Which of the following statements is/are TRUE?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} Every subset of a recursively enumerable language is recursive. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} If a language $L$ and its complement $\overline{L}$ are both recursively enumerable, then $L$ must be recursive. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} Complement of a context-free language must be recursive. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} If $L_1$ and $L_2$ are regular, then $L_1 \cap L_2$ must be deterministic context-free.
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B,C,D} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Theory of Computation \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Regular and context-free languages, pumping lemma}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small If $L$ and $\overline{L}$ are both recursively enumerable, $L$ is recursive by Post's theorem (B is TRUE). Every CFL is recursive, and recursive languages are closed under complement, so $\overline{\text{CFL}}$ is recursive (C is TRUE). Regular languages are closed under intersection, and every regular language is a DCFL (D is TRUE). Subsets of RE languages need not be recursive (A is FALSE).}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[
    blanker,
    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,
    middle=16pt,
    underlay={

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.132} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2023 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2023\_CS\_CS\_Q24}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Which of the following statements is/are CORRECT?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} The intersection of two regular languages is regular. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} The intersection of two context-free languages is context-free. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} The intersection of two recursive languages is recursive. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} The intersection of two recursively enumerable languages is recursively enumerable.
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A, C, D} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Theory of Computation \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Regular and context-free languages, pumping lemma}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Regular languages, recursive languages, and recursively enumerable languages are all closed under intersection (A, C, and D are CORRECT). Context-free languages are not closed under intersection; for example, $L_1 = \{a^n b^n c^m\}$ and $L_2 = \{a^m b^n c^n\}$ are both CFLs, but $L_1 \cap L_2 = \{a^n b^n c^n\}$ is not context-free.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[
    blanker,
    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,
    middle=16pt,
    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.133} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS1\_CS\_Q23}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Let $L_1, L_2$ be two regular languages and $L_3$ a language which is not regular. Which of the following statements is/are always TRUE?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} $L_1 = L_2$ if and only if $L_1 \cap \overline{L_2} = \emptyset$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} $L_1 \cup L_3$ is not regular \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} $\overline{L_3}$ is not regular \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} $\overline{L_1} \cup \overline{L_2}$ is regular
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large C;D} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Theory of Computation \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Regular and context-free languages, pumping lemma}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Regular languages are closed under complementation and union; since $L_1, L_2$ are regular, $\overline{L_1}$ and $\overline{L_2}$ are regular, so $\overline{L_1} \cup \overline{L_2}$ is regular (D is TRUE). If $L_3$ is non-regular, its complement $\overline{L_3}$ must also be non-regular, because if $\overline{L_3}$ were regular, its complement $\overline{\overline{L_3}} = L_3$ would be regular, a contradiction (C is TRUE).}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[

    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,
    middle=16pt,
    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.134} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS1\_CS\_Q19}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider the following context-free grammar $G$, where $S, A$, and $B$ are the variables (non-terminals), $a$ and $b$ are the terminal symbols, $S$ is the start variable, and the rules of $G$ are described as:
\[ S \to aaB \mid Abb \]
\[ A \to a \mid aA \]
\[ B \to b \mid bB \]
Which ONE of the languages $L(G)$ is accepted by $G$?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} $L(G) = \{a^2b^n \mid n \ge 1\} \cup \{a^nb^2 \mid n \ge 1\}$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} $L(G) = \{a^nb^{2n} \mid n \ge 1\} \cup \{a^{2n}b^n \mid n \ge 1\}$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} $L(G) = \{a^nb^n \mid n \ge 1\}$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} $L(G) = \{a^{2n}b^{2n} \mid n \ge 1\}$
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Theory of Computation \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Context-free grammars and push-down automata}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small From the rules, $A$ generates $a^+$ (any $a^n$ with $n \ge 1$) and $B$ generates $b^+$ (any $b^n$ with $n \ge 1$). Thus, $aaB$ produces $a^2b^n$ ($n \ge 1$), and $Abb$ produces $a^nb^2$ ($n \ge 1$). Therefore, $L(G) = \{a^2b^n \mid n \ge 1\} \cup \{a^nb^2 \mid n \ge 1\}$.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[

\textbf{Q.135} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS1\_CS\_Q28}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

A regular language $L$ is accepted by a non-deterministic finite automaton (NFA) with $n$ states. Which of the following statement(s) is/are FALSE?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} $L$ may have an accepting NFA with $< n$ states. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} $L$ may have an accepting DFA with $< n$ states. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} There exists a DFA with $\le 2^n$ states that accepts $L$. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} Every DFA that accepts $L$ has $> 2^n$ states.
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large D} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Theory of Computation \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Regular expressions and finite automata}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small By the standard subset construction algorithm, every $n$-state NFA can be converted into an equivalent deterministic finite automaton (DFA) having at most $2^n$ states. Statement D asserts that every DFA must have strictly more than $2^n$ states, which is directly false.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[
    blanker,
    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,
    middle=16pt,
    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.136} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS2\_CS\_Q24}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Which ONE of the following languages is accepted by a deterministic pushdown automaton?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} Any regular language. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} Any context-free language. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} Any language accepted by a non-deterministic pushdown automaton. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} Any decidable language.
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Theory of Computation \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Context-free grammars and push-down automata}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Every regular language is recognized by a Deterministic Finite Automaton (DFA), which can be directly simulated by a DPDA without using stack operations.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[

    sidebyside align=top,

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);

]
\textbf{Q.137} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS2\_CS\_Q25}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Let $G_1, G_2$ be Context Free Grammars (CFGs) and $R$ be a regular expression. For a grammar $G$, let $L(G)$ denote the language generated by $G$. Which ONE among the following questions is decidable?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} Is $L(G_1) = L(G_2)$? \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} Is $L(G_1) \cap L(G_2) = \emptyset$? \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} Is $L(G_1) = L(R)$? \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} Is $L(G_1) = \emptyset$?
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large D} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Theory of Computation \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Turing machines and undecidability}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small For Context-Free Grammars, emptiness ($L(G) = \emptyset$) is decidable via finding generating non-terminals, whereas equivalence ($L(G_1) = L(G_2)$) and disjointness ($L(G_1) \cap L(G_2) = \emptyset$) are undecidable.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.138} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS2\_CS\_Q30}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider the two lists List I and List II given below:
\par\medskip
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{ll}
\toprule
List I & List II \\
\midrule
(i) Context free languages & (a) Closed under union \\
(ii) Recursive languages & (b) Not closed under complementation \\
(iii) Regular languages & (c) Closed under intersection \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}\end{center}
\par\medskip
For matching of items in List I with those in List II, which of the following option(s) is/are CORRECT?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} (i) – (a), (ii) – (b), and (iii) – (c) \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} (i) – (b), (ii) – (a), and (iii) – (c) \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} (i) – (b), (ii) – (c), and (iii) – (a) \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} (i) – (a), (ii) – (c), and (iii) – (b)
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B;C} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Theory of Computation \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Regular and context-free languages: closure properties}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Context-free languages are not closed under complementation (i - b) or intersection, but are closed under union. Recursive languages are closed under union and complementation and intersection (ii - a or c). Regular languages are closed under all three.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

\textbf{Q.139} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2018 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2018\_CS\_GA\_Q08\_v2}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Which one of the following statements is FALSE?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} Context-free grammar can be used to specify both lexical and syntax rules. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} Type checking is done before parsing. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} High-level language programs can be converted to intermediate code directly. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} Arguments to a function can be passed using the program stack.
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Compiler Design \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Lexical analysis, parsing, syntax-directed translation}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Type checking verifies semantic consistency according to language type rules and is performed during semantic analysis, which occurs after syntax analysis (parsing). Therefore, the statement that type checking is done during syntax analysis is FALSE.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.140} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2022 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2022\_CS\_CS\_Q13}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Which one of the following statements is TRUE?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} The LALR(1) parser for a grammar G cannot have reduce-reduce conflict if the LR(1) parser for G does not have reduce-reduce conflict. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} Symbol table is accessed only during the lexical analysis phase. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} Data flow analysis is necessary for run-time memory management. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} LR(1) parsing is sufficient for deterministic context-free languages.
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large D} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Compiler Design \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Lexical analysis, parsing, syntax-directed translation}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small LR(1) grammars describe exactly the family of Deterministic Context-Free Languages (DCFLs), meaning LR(1) parsing is both necessary and sufficient for any DCFL. LALR(1) can introduce reduce-reduce conflicts not present in LR(1), and symbol tables are accessed throughout compilation.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[
    blanker,
    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.141} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[NAT $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2022 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2022\_CS\_CS\_Q29}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider the augmented grammar with $\{+, *, (, ), id\}$ as the set of terminals:
\begin{align*}
S' &\to S \\
S &\to S + R \mid R \\
R &\to R * P \mid P \\
P &\to ( S ) \mid id
\end{align*}
If $I_0$ is the set of two LR(0) items $\{[S' \to \bullet S], [S \to \bullet S + R]\}$, then $\text{goto}(\text{closure}(I_0), +)$ contains exactly \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_ items. (Answer in integer)

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent\textbf{Your Answer:} \fbox{\rule{0pt}{1.3ex}\hspace{3cm}}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large 5 to 5} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Compiler Design \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Lexical analysis, parsing, syntax-directed translation}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small From item $[S \to S \bullet + R]$ in $\text{closure}(I_0)$, transitioning on symbol $+$ yields the kernel item $[S \to S + \bullet R]$. Computing its closure adds $[R \to \bullet R * P]$, $[R \to \bullet P]$, $[P \to \bullet ( S )]$, and $[P \to \bullet id]$, resulting in exactly 5 items.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[

    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,

    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.142} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2023 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2023\_CS\_CS\_Q36}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider the following program:
\begin{lstlisting}[language=C]
int main() {
    f1();
    f2(2);
    f3();
    return(0);
}
int f1() {
    return(1);
}
int f2(int X) {
    f3();
    if (X == 1)
        return f1();
    else
        return (X * f2(X - 1));
}
int f3() {
    return(5);
}
\end{lstlisting}
Which one of the following options represents the activation tree corresponding to the main function?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} main -> [f1, f2, f3]; where f2(2) -> [f3, f2(1)]; and f2(1) -> [f3, f1] \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} main -> [f2, f3]; where f2 -> [f1, f1, f3] \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} main -> [f2]; where f2 -> [f1, f3, f1] \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} main -> [f1, f2, f3]; where f2 -> [f3, f2, f1]
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Compiler Design \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Runtime environments}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small In `main()`, the calls are `f1()`, `f2(2)`, and `f3()` as direct children. Inside `f2(2)`, it invokes `f3()` first, then because $X=2 \neq 1$, recursively invokes `f2(1)`. Inside `f2(1)`, it invokes `f3()` first, and then since $X=1$, invokes `f1()`. This call sequence maps directly to the tree structure in option A.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.143} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS1\_CS\_Q26}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Which of the following is/are Bottom-Up Parser(s)?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} Shift-reduce Parser & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} Predictive Parser \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} LL(1) Parser & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} LR Parser
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A;D} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Compiler Design \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Lexical analysis, parsing, syntax-directed translation}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Bottom-up parsers reduce an input string back to the start symbol by finding handles. Shift-reduce parsers and LR parsers (SLR, CLR, LALR) are bottom-up parsers. In contrast, Predictive and LL(1) parsers construct the derivation tree from the root downward and are top-down parsers.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.144} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[NAT $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS1\_CS\_Q33}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider the operator precedence and associativity rules for the integer arithmetic operators given in the table below:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{|c|c|c|}
\hline
\textbf{Operator} & \textbf{Precedence} & \textbf{Associativity} \\
\hline
$+$ & Highest & Left \\
$-$ & High & Right \\
$*$ & Medium & Right \\
$/$ & Low & Right \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The value of the expression $3 + 1 + 5 * 2 / 7 + 2 - 4 - 7 - 6 / 2$ as per the above rules is \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\. (Answer in integer)

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent\textbf{Your Answer:} \fbox{\rule{0pt}{1.3ex}\hspace{3cm}}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large 6 to 6} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Compiler Design \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Lexical analysis, parsing, syntax-directed translation}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Precedence order is $+$ (highest, left-associative), then $-$ (right-associative), then $*$ (right-associative), then $/$ (lowest, right-associative). Evaluating $+$ first: $3+1+5 = 9$ and $7+2 = 9$. Expression becomes $9 * 2 / 9 - 4 - 7 - 6 / 2$. Evaluating $-$ right-associatively: $4 - (7 - 6) = 4 - 1 = 3$, giving $9 - 3 = 6$. The expression becomes $9 * 2 / 6 / 2$. Next $*$ gives $18 / 6 / 2$. Evaluating $/$ right-associatively: $6 / 2 = 3$, and $18 / 3 = 6$.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[

    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,
    middle=16pt,
    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.145} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS2\_CS\_Q21}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider the following two sets:
\par\medskip
\begin{tabular}{ll}
\textbf{Set X} & \textbf{Set Y} \\
P. Lexical Analyzer & 1. Abstract Syntax Tree \\
Q. Syntax Analyzer & 2. Token \\
R. Intermediate Code Generator & 3. Parse Tree \\
S. Code Optimizer & 4. Constant Folding
\end{tabular}
\par\medskip
Which one of the following options is the CORRECT match from Set X to Set Y?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} P -- 4; Q -- 1; R -- 3; S -- 2 & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} P -- 2; Q -- 3; R -- 1; S -- 4 \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} P -- 2; Q -- 1; R -- 3; S -- 4 & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} P -- 4; Q -- 3; R -- 2; S -- 1
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Compiler Design \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Lexical analysis, parsing, syntax-directed translation}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Lexical Analyzer outputs tokens (P - 2). Syntax Analyzer constructs parse trees (Q - 3). Intermediate Code Generator generates abstract syntax trees or intermediate representations (R - 1). Code Optimizer performs constant folding (S - 4). Thus, the correct matching is P - 2; Q - 3; R - 1; S - 4.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.146} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS2\_CS\_Q29}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Which of the following statements is/are FALSE?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} An attribute grammar is a syntax-directed definition (SDD) in which the functions in the semantic rules have no side effects \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} The attributes in a L-attributed definition cannot always be evaluated in a depth-first order \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} Synthesized attributes can be evaluated by a bottom-up parser as the input is parsed \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} All L-attributed definitions based on LR(1) grammar can be evaluated using a bottom-up parsing strategy
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B;D} \hfill \textit{\small [Hard]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Compiler Design \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Lexical analysis, parsing, syntax-directed translation}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small L-attributed definitions are explicitly defined such that their attributes can always be evaluated via depth-first (left-to-right) traversal (making statement B false). Not all L-attributed definitions on LR(1) grammars can be evaluated during bottom-up parsing without rewriting or marker nonterminals (making statement D false).}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[

    sidebyside,

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.147} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS1\_CS\_Q12}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Which ONE of the following statements is FALSE regarding the symbol table?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} Symbol table is responsible for keeping track of the scope of variables. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} Symbol table can be implemented using a binary search tree. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} Symbol table is not required after the parsing phase. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} Symbol table is created during the lexical analysis phase.
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large C} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Compiler Design \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Lexical analysis, parsing, syntax-directed translation}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small The symbol table is actively used throughout all phases of the compiler, including semantic analysis, intermediate code generation, code optimization, and target code generation for type checking, memory allocation, and address resolution. Thus, claiming it is not required after parsing is false.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

]
\textbf{Q.148} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS1\_CS\_Q13}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Which ONE of the following techniques used in compiler code optimization uses live variable analysis?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} Run-time function call management \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} Register assignment to variables \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} Strength reduction \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} Constant folding
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Compiler Design \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Data flow analyses: constant propagation, liveness analysis, common sub expression elimination}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Live variable analysis determines whether the value in a variable could be read along some execution path before being redefined. This information is directly used in register allocation and assignment to construct interference graphs and allocate registers efficiently.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

]
\textbf{Q.149} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS2\_CS\_Q21}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider the following statements about the use of backpatching in a compiler for intermediate code generation:
(I) Backpatching can be used to generate code for Boolean expression in one pass.
(II) Backpatching can be used to generate code for flow-of-control statements in one pass.
Which ONE of the following options is CORRECT?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} Only (I) is correct. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} Only (II) is correct. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} Both (I) and (II) are correct. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} Neither (I) nor (II) is correct.
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large C} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Compiler Design \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Intermediate code generation and backpatching}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Backpatching is a standard technique used in single-pass compilers to synthesize target labels for forward jumps in both Boolean expressions and flow-of-control statements.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[

\textbf{Q.150} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS2\_CS\_Q22}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Given the following syntax directed translation rules:
- Rule 1: $R \to AB \;\{ B.i = R.i - 1; \; A.i = B.i; \; R.i = A.i + 1; \}$
- Rule 2: $P \to CD \;\{ P.i = C.i + D.i; \; D.i = C.i + 2; \}$
- Rule 3: $Q \to EF \;\{ Q.i = E.i + F.i; \}$
\par\medskip
Which ONE is the CORRECT option among the following?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} Rule 1 is S-attributed and L-attributed; Rule 2 is S-attributed and not L-attributed; Rule 3 is neither S-attributed nor L-attributed \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} Rule 1 is neither S-attributed nor L-attributed; Rule 2 is S-attributed and L-attributed; Rule 3 is S-attributed and L-attributed \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} Rule 1 is neither S-attributed nor L-attributed; Rule 2 is not S-attributed and is L-attributed; Rule 3 is S-attributed and L-attributed \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} Rule 1 is S-attributed and not L-attributed; Rule 2 is not S-attributed and is L-attributed; Rule 3 is S-attributed and L-attributed
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large C} \hfill \textit{\small [Hard]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Compiler Design \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Syntax-directed translation: S-attributed and L-attributed definitions}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small In Rule 1, $R.i$ is computed from children (synthesized-like) but defined circularly with inherited attributes, making it neither. In Rule 2, attributes depend only on left siblings, satisfying L-attributed definition. In Rule 3, $Q.i$ depends only on children, which is S-attributed (and hence L-attributed).}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

\subsection*{Operating Systems}
\vspace{-4pt}
\noindent
