\textbf{Q.151} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2018 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2018\_CS\_GA\_Q10\_v2}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider a process executing on an operating system that uses demand paging. The average time for a memory access in the system is $M$ units if the corresponding memory page is available in memory, and $D$ units $(D \gg M)$ if the page needs to be fetched from the disk. If $X$ is the effective memory access time, the page fault rate $p$ is

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} $\frac{X - M}{D}$ & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} $\frac{X - M}{D - M}$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} $\frac{D - X}{D - M}$ & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} $\frac{X - M}{D + M}$
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Operating System \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Memory management and virtual memory}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small The effective access time is $X = (1 - p)M + pD = M + p(D - M)$. Solving for $p$ yields $p(D - M) = X - M \implies p = \frac{X - M}{D - M}$.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[
    blanker,
    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,
    middle=16pt,
    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.152} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[NAT $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2018 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2018\_CS\_GA\_Q24}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider a system with 3 processes that share 4 instances of the same resource type. Each process can request at most $K$ instances of the resource type at any time. The largest value of $K$ that will always avoid deadlock is \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_.

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent\textbf{Your Answer:} \fbox{\rule{0pt}{1.3ex}\hspace{3cm}}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large 2 to 2} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Operating System \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Deadlock}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small For $n$ processes sharing $m$ instances of a single resource type where each process needs at most $K$ instances, deadlock is guaranteed to be avoided if $\sum_{i=1}^n (K - 1) < m \implies n(K - 1) \le m - 1$. Here $n = 3$ and $m = 4$, so $3(K - 1) \le 3 \implies K - 1 \le 1 \implies K \le 2$.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[
    blanker,
    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,
    middle=16pt,
    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.153} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2022 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2022\_CS\_CS\_Q19}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider the following threads, T1, T2, and T3 executing on a single processor, synchronized using three binary semaphore variables, S1, S2, and S3, operated upon using standard `wait()` and `signal()`:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{p{3.5cm}p{3.5cm}p{3.5cm}}
\textbf{T1} & \textbf{T2} & \textbf{T3} \\
\texttt{while(true)\{} & \texttt{while(true)\{} & \texttt{while(true)\{} \\
\texttt{\ \ wait(S3);} & \texttt{\ \ wait(S1);} & \texttt{\ \ wait(S2);} \\
\texttt{\ \ print("C");} & \texttt{\ \ print("B");} & \texttt{\ \ print("A");} \\
\texttt{\ \ signal(S2);\}} & \texttt{\ \ signal(S3);\}} & \texttt{\ \ signal(S1);\}}
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
Which initialization of the semaphores would print the sequence BCABCABCA\dots?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} $S_1 = 1; S_2 = 1; S_3 = 1$ & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} $S_1 = 1; S_2 = 1; S_3 = 0$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} $S_1 = 1; S_2 = 0; S_3 = 0$ & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} $S_1 = 0; S_2 = 1; S_3 = 1$
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large C} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Operating System \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Concurrency and synchronization}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small To output BCABCABCA..., the sequence begins with 'B' from T2, which requires $S_1 = 1$. After printing 'B', T2 signals $S_3$, allowing T1 to print 'C'. T1 then signals $S_2$, allowing T3 to print 'A'. Finally, T3 signals $S_1$, enabling T2 again. Therefore, the required initial semaphore values are $S_1 = 1, S_2 = 0, S_3 = 0$.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[
    blanker,
    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,
    middle=16pt,
    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.154} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2022 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2022\_CS\_CS\_Q26}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Which of the following statements is/are TRUE with respect to deadlocks?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} Circular wait is a necessary condition for the formation of deadlock. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} In a system where each resource has more than one instance, a cycle in its wait-for graph indicates the presence of a deadlock. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} If the current allocation of resources to processes leads the system to unsafe state, then deadlock will necessarily occur. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} In the resource-allocation graph of a system, if every edge is an assignment edge, then the system is not in deadlock state.
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A,D} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Operating System \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Deadlock}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Circular wait is one of Coffman's four necessary conditions for deadlock (A is TRUE). If every edge in a resource-allocation graph is an assignment edge, no process is requesting any resource, so no process is blocked and deadlock is impossible (D is TRUE). In multi-instance systems, a cycle is not sufficient for deadlock, and unsafe states do not guarantee deadlock.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[
    blanker,
    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,
    middle=16pt,
    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.155} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2023 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2023\_CS\_CS\_Q22}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Which one or more of the following need to be saved on a context switch from one thread (T1) of a process to another thread (T2) of the same process?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} Page table base register & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} Stack pointer \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} Program counter & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} General purpose registers
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B, C, D} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Operating System \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{System calls, processes, threads, inter-process communication}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Threads of the same process share the process address space, open files, and the page table base register (PTBR is not switched). However, each thread has its own private thread control block (TCB) maintaining its program counter, stack pointer, and general purpose CPU registers, which must be saved during a thread context switch.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[
    blanker,
    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,
    middle=16pt,
    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.156} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2023 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2023\_CS\_CS\_Q23}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Which one or more of the following options guarantee that a computer system will transition from user mode to kernel mode?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} Function Call & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} `malloc` Call \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} Page Fault & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} System Call
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large C, D} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Operating System \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{System calls, processes, threads, inter-process communication}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small A transition to kernel mode is triggered by traps, exceptions, or hardware interrupts. A Page Fault is a hardware exception handled exclusively by the OS kernel (C is true). A System Call issues an explicit software trap instruction switching the CPU to kernel mode (D is true). Standard user-space function calls and `malloc` execute in user mode without inherently causing a mode transition.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

\textbf{Q.157} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2023 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2023\_CS\_CS\_Q27}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Which one or more of the following CPU scheduling algorithms can potentially cause starvation?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} First-in First-Out & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} Round Robin \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} Priority Scheduling & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} Shortest Job First
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A, C, D or C, D} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Operating System \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{CPU and I/O scheduling}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Priority Scheduling can cause starvation for low-priority processes if higher-priority processes arrive continuously (C). Shortest Job First (SJF) starves long burst jobs when short jobs arrive continuously (D). Under certain interpretations where FIFO queues suffer convoy effects or indefinitely waiting jobs in specific priority variants, FIFO was also credited, hence GATE accepted {C, D} or {A, C, D}. Round Robin provides bounded waiting and prevents starvation.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.158} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS1\_CS\_Q24}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Which of the following statements about threads is/are TRUE?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} Threads can only be implemented in kernel space \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} Each thread has its own file descriptor table for open files \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} All the threads belonging to a process share a common stack \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} Threads belonging to a process are by default not protected from each other
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large D} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Operating System \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{System calls, processes, threads, inter-process communication}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small All threads of a single process share the same address space (code, global data, heap) and open files. Because they run within the same virtual address space without memory protection barriers between them, one thread can corrupt another thread's memory; hence they are by default not protected from each other (D is TRUE). Each thread maintains its own private stack and registers.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.159} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS1\_CS\_Q25}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Which of the following process state transitions is/are NOT possible?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} Running to Ready & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} Waiting to Running \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} Ready to Waiting & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} Running to Terminated
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B;C} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Operating System \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{System calls, processes, threads, inter-process communication}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small In the standard process state model, a process in the Waiting (Blocked) state transitions to Ready upon event/I/O completion; it cannot transition directly to Running without CPU dispatch (B is impossible). Similarly, only a Running process can initiate an I/O request and transition to Waiting; a Ready process cannot transition directly to Waiting (C is impossible).}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --

    }
]
\textbf{Q.160} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS2\_CS\_Q24}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Which of the following tasks is/are the responsibility/responsibilities of the memory management unit (MMU) in a system with paging-based memory management?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} Allocate a new page table for a newly created process \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} Translate a virtual address to a physical address using the page table \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} Raise a trap when a virtual address is not found in the page table \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} Raise a trap when a process tries to write to a page marked with read-only permission in the page table
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B;C;D} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Operating System \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Memory management and virtual memory}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small The MMU hardware translates virtual addresses to physical addresses using page tables/TLB (B), generates a page-fault trap when a page table entry is invalid (C), and raises a protection trap on access permission violations such as writing to read-only pages (D). Page table allocation (A) is an OS kernel duty.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --

    }
]
\textbf{Q.161} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS2\_CS\_Q25}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider a process P running on a CPU. Which one or more of the following events will always trigger a context switch by the OS that results in process P moving to a non-running state (e.g., ready, blocked)?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} P makes a blocking system call to read a block of data from the disk \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} P tries to access a page that is in the swap space, triggering a page fault \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} An interrupt is raised by the disk to deliver data requested by some other process \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} A timer interrupt is raised by the hardware
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A;B} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Operating System \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{System calls, processes, threads, inter-process communication}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small When process P executes a blocking I/O system call, it must wait for disk transfer and is moved to the blocked state (A). When P experiences a page fault on a swapped page, it is put into the blocked state until disk I/O completes (B). Interrupts from other devices (C) or timer ticks (D) in preemptive systems might return directly to P after ISR handling if P has highest priority.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[

    sidebyside,

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.162} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS1\_CS\_Q14}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider a demand paging memory management system with 32-bit logical address, 20-bit physical address, and page size of 2048 bytes. Assuming that the memory is byte addressable, what is the maximum number of entries in the page table?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} $2^{21}$ & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} $2^{20}$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} $2^{22}$ & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} $2^{24}$
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Operating System \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Memory management and virtual memory}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small With a page size of $2048 = 2^{11}$ bytes, the page offset requires 11 bits. The 32-bit logical address is split into page number and page offset, so the page number has $32 - 11 = 21$ bits. Hence, the page table contains at most $2^{21}$ entries.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[

    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,

    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.163} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[NAT $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS1\_CS\_Q29}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Suppose in a multiprogramming environment, the following C program segment is executed. A process goes into I/O queue whenever an I/O related operation is performed. Assume that there will always be a context switch whenever a process requests for an I/O, and also whenever the process returns from an I/O. The number of times the process will enter the ready queue during its lifetime (not counting the time the process enters the ready queue when it is run initially) is \_\_\_\_\_\_\. (Answer in integer)
\par\medskip
\begin{verbatim}
int main()
{
    int x=0, i=0;
    scanf("%d", &x);
    for(i=0; i<20; i++)
    {
        x = x + 20;
        printf("%d\n", x);
    }
    return 0;
}
\end{verbatim}

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent\textbf{Your Answer:} \fbox{\rule{0pt}{1.3ex}\hspace{3cm}}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large 21 to 21} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Operating System \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{CPU and I/O scheduling}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small There is 1 initial `scanf` and 20 iterations of `printf`, totaling $1 + 20 = 21$ I/O operations. Each time an I/O operation completes, the process transitions from the I/O (waiting) queue back into the ready queue. Excluding the initial program launch into the ready queue, the process enters the ready queue exactly 21 times.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.164} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS2\_CS\_Q26}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Processes $P_1, P_2, P_3, P_4$ arrive in that order at times 0, 1, 2, and 8 milliseconds respectively, and have execution times of 10, 13, 6, and 9 milliseconds respectively. Shortest Remaining Time First (SRTF) algorithm is used as the CPU scheduling policy. Ignore context switching times. Which ONE of the following correctly gives the average turnaround time of the four processes in milliseconds?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} 22 & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} 15 \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} 37 & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} 19
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large D} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Operating System \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{CPU and I/O scheduling: SRTF}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Using preemptive SRTF: $P_1$ runs [0,2], $P_3$ runs [2,8] (finishes at 8, TAT = $8-2=6$). Then $P_4$ (remaining 9) runs [8,17] (finishes at 17, TAT = $17-8=9$). $P_1$ (remaining 8) runs [17,25] (finishes at 25, TAT = $25-0=25$). $P_2$ runs [25,38] (finishes at 38, TAT = $38-1=37$). Average TAT = $\frac{25 + 37 + 6 + 9}{4} = \frac{76}{4} = 19$ ms.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

\textbf{Q.165} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2018 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2018\_CS\_GA\_Q11}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

In an Entity-Relationship (ER) model, suppose $R$ is a many-to-one relationship from entity set $E_1$ to entity set $E_2$. Assume that $E_1$ has total participation in $R$, but $E_2$ has partial participation in $R$. Which one of the following statements is necessarily TRUE?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} Every entity in $E_1$ is associated with exactly one entity in $E_2$. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} Some entity in $E_1$ is associated with more than one entity in $E_2$. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} Every entity in $E_2$ is associated with at least one entity in $E_1$. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} Every entity in $E_2$ is associated with at most one entity in $E_1$.
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Databases \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{ER-model}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small In an ER diagram, a many-to-one relationship from $E_1$ to $E_2$ means each entity in $E_1$ is related to at most one entity in $E_2$. Total participation of $E_1$ requires that every entity in $E_1$ participates in at least one relationship. Combining these, every entity in $E_1$ is associated with exactly one entity in $E_2$.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[

    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,

    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.166} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2018 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2018\_CS\_GA\_Q12}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider the following two tables and four queries in SQL.
\texttt{Book (isbn, bname)}
\texttt{Stock (isbn, copies)}
\par\medskip
Query 1: \texttt{SELECT B.isbn, S.copies FROM Book B INNER JOIN Stock S ON B.isbn = S.isbn;}
Query 2: \texttt{SELECT B.isbn, S.copies FROM Book B LEFT OUTER JOIN Stock S ON B.isbn = S.isbn;}
Query 3: \texttt{SELECT B.isbn, S.copies FROM Book B RIGHT OUTER JOIN Stock S ON B.isbn = S.isbn;}
Query 4: \texttt{SELECT B.isbn, S.copies FROM Book B FULL OUTER JOIN Stock S ON B.isbn = S.isbn;}
\par\medskip
Which one of the following statements is CORRECT?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} Query 1 and Query 2 always produce the same number of rows. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} Query 2 and Query 3 always produce the same number of rows. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} Query 1 always produces the maximum number of rows among all queries. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} All rows from Query 1, Query 2, and Query 3 are always present in the result of Query 4.
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large D} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Databases \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Relational model: relational algebra, tuple calculus, SQL}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Query 1 uses natural join (inner join), Query 2 uses left outer join, Query 3 uses right outer join, and Query 4 uses full outer join. A full outer join preserves all tuples from both relation $Book$ and relation $Stock$ (filling missing values with NULLs). Therefore, every tuple output by Query 1, Query 2, and Query 3 is guaranteed to appear in Query 4.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.167} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2022 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2022\_CS\_CS\_Q14}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

In a relational data model, which one of the following statements is TRUE?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} A relation with only two attributes is always in BCNF. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} If all attributes of a relation are prime attributes, then the relation is in BCNF. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} Every relation has at least one non-prime attribute. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} BCNF decompositions preserve functional dependencies.
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Databases \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Integrity constraints, normal forms}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small For any binary relation $R(A, B)$, the only possible non-trivial functional dependencies are $A \to B$ or $B \to A$. In either case, the left-hand side is a superkey of $R$, satisfying the definition of Boyce-Codd Normal Form (BCNF).}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[
    blanker,
    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,
    middle=16pt,
    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.168} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2022 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2022\_CS\_CS\_Q25}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider the following three relations in a relational database:
\[ \text{Employee}(eId, Name), \quad \text{Brand}(bId, bName), \quad \text{Own}(eId, bId) \]
Which of the following relational algebra expressions return the set of $eIds$ who own all the brands?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} $\Pi_{eId}(\Pi_{eId, bId}(Own) / \Pi_{bId}(Brand))$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} $\Pi_{eId}(Own) - \Pi_{eId}((\Pi_{eId}(Own) \times \Pi_{bId}(Brand)) - Own)$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} $\Pi_{eId}(\Pi_{eId, bId}(Own) / \Pi_{bId}(Own))$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} $\Pi_{eId}((\Pi_{eId}(Own) \times \Pi_{bId}(Brand)) / \Pi_{bId}(Own))$
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A,B} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Databases \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Relational model: relational algebra, tuple calculus, SQL}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Relational division $\Pi_{eId, bId}(Own) / \Pi_{bId}(Brand)$ directly computes employees who own all brands (A is TRUE). Equivalently, taking all employees, finding those missing at least one brand via cartesian product with Brand followed by set difference with Own, and subtracting them from all employees yields the same set (B is TRUE).}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[
    blanker,
    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,
    middle=16pt,
    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.169} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[NAT $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2022 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2022\_CS\_CS\_Q31}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider a relation $R(A, B, C, D, E)$ with the following three functional dependencies:
\[ AB \to C; \quad BC \to D; \quad C \to E; \]
The number of superkeys in the relation $R$ is \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\. (Answer in integer)

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent\textbf{Your Answer:} \fbox{\rule{0pt}{1.3ex}\hspace{3cm}}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large 8 to 8} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Databases \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Integrity constraints, normal forms}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Computing attribute closures: $(AB)^+ = \{A, B, C, D, E\}$. Attributes $A$ and $B$ never appear on any RHS, so $AB$ is the unique candidate key. Every superkey must contain $AB$, and can include any subset of the remaining 3 attributes $\{C, D, E\}$, giving $2^3 = 8$ superkeys.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.170} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2023 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2023\_CS\_CS\_Q16}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Which one of the options given below refers to the degree (or arity) of a relation in relational database systems?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} Number of attributes of its relation schema. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} Number of tuples stored in the relation. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} Number of entries in the relation. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} Number of distinct domains of its relation schema.
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Databases \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Relational model: relational algebra, tuple calculus, SQL}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small In relational database theory, the degree (or arity) of a relation is defined as the number of attributes (columns) in its relation schema, whereas the cardinality of a relation is the number of tuples (rows).}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

]

\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Let $S$ be the specification: ``Instructors teach courses. Students register for courses. Courses are allocated classrooms. Instructors guide students.'' Which one of the following ER diagrams CORRECTLY represents $S$?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} (i) & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} (ii) \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} (iii) & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} (iv)
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large D} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Databases \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{ER-model}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Specification $S$ describes four distinct binary relationships among entities: Instructors teach courses, Students register for courses, Courses are allocated classrooms, and Instructors guide students. In standard ER modeling, entity sets are represented by rectangles and relationships by diamond diamonds connecting them; diagram (iv) correctly captures all four relationship sets.}

\end{tcolorbox}

\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

In a $\text{B}^+$ tree, the requirement of at least half-full (50\%) node occupancy is relaxed for which one of the following cases?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} Only the root node & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} All leaf nodes \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} All internal nodes & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} Only the leftmost leaf node
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Databases \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{File organization, indexing (e.g., B and B+ trees)}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small In a $\text{B}^+$ tree, every node except the root node must satisfy the minimum occupancy constraint of being at least half full ($	ext{at least } \lceil p/2 \rceil$ child pointers). The root node is the sole exception, and is permitted to hold as few as two pointers (or one key) when non-leaf, and one key when leaf.}

\end{tcolorbox}

\textbf{Q.173} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS1\_CS\_Q22}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Which of the following statements about a relation $R$ in first normal form (1NF) is/are TRUE?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} $R$ can have a multi-attribute key \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} $R$ cannot have a foreign key \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} $R$ cannot have a composite attribute \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} $R$ cannot have more than one candidate key
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A;C} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Databases \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Integrity constraints, normal forms}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small A relation is in 1NF if and only if all attribute values are atomic, which explicitly forbids composite or multi-valued attributes (making (C) TRUE). 1NF places no limitation on candidate keys having multiple attributes (multi-attribute keys are fully permitted, making (A) TRUE) or the presence of foreign keys.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --

    }
]
\textbf{Q.174} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[NAT $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS1\_CS\_Q35}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider the following two relations, $R(A,B)$ and $S(A,C)$:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{|c|c|}
\multicolumn{2}{c}{$R$} \\
\hline
$A$ & $B$ \\
\hline
10 & 20 \\
20 & 30 \\
30 & 40 \\
30 & 50 \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\qquad
\begin{tabular}{|c|c|}
\multicolumn{2}{c}{$S$} \\
\hline
$A$ & $C$ \\
\hline
10 & 90 \\
30 & 45 \\
40 & 80 \\
50 & 95 \\
\hline
\end{tabular}
\end{center}
The total number of tuples obtained by evaluating the following expression
\[ \sigma\_{B < C}(R \bowtie\_{R.A = S.A} S) \]
is \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\. (Answer in integer)

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent\textbf{Your Answer:} \fbox{\rule{0pt}{1.3ex}\hspace{3cm}}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large 2 to 2} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Databases \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Relational model: relational algebra, tuple calculus, SQL}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Joining $R(A,B)$ and $S(A,C)$ on $R.A = S.A$ yields: $(10, 20, 90)$ for $A=10$, and $(30, 40, 45)$, $(30, 50, 45)$ for $A=30$. Applying selection $\sigma_{B < C}$: $20 < 90$ is true (tuple $(10, 20, 90)$), $40 < 45$ is true (tuple $(30, 40, 45)$), and $50 < 45$ is false. Thus, exactly 2 tuples satisfy the condition.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.175} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS2\_CS\_Q19}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Once the DBMS informs the user that a transaction has been successfully completed, its effect should persist even if the system crashes before all its changes are reflected on disk. This property is called:

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} durability & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} atomicity \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} consistency & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} isolation
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Databases \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Transactions and concurrency control}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small The Durability property of the ACID paradigm guarantees that once a transaction has committed successfully, its updates and effects survive permanently in non-volatile storage, even in the event of a subsequent system crash.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

\textbf{Q.176} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS2\_CS\_Q20}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

In the context of owner and weak entity sets in the ER (Entity-Relationship) data model, which one of the following statements is TRUE?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} The weak entity set MUST have total participation in the identifying relationship \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} The owner entity set MUST have total participation in the identifying relationship \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} Both weak and owner entity sets MUST have total participation in the identifying relationship \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} Neither weak entity set nor owner entity set MUST have total participation in the identifying relationship
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Databases \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{ER-model}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small A weak entity cannot exist without its identifying (owner) entity. Therefore, every instance of the weak entity set must be associated with an owner entity instance via the identifying relationship, mandating total participation of the weak entity set.}

\end{tcolorbox}

\textbf{Q.177} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS2\_CS\_Q26}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Which of the following file organizations is/are I/O efficient for the scan operation in DBMS?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} Sorted & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} Heap \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} Unclustered tree index & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} Unclustered hash index
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A;B} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Databases \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{File organization, indexing (e.g., B and B+ trees)}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small A full table scan retrieves all pages sequentially. Sorted and Heap files store data records densely packed across consecutive data pages, requiring reading each page exactly once. Unclustered indexes store data out of order, incurring multiple random I/Os per record.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

\begin{tcolorbox}[

]
\textbf{Q.178} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS2\_CS\_Q27}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Which of the following statements about the Two Phase Locking (2PL) protocol is/are TRUE?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} 2PL permits only serializable schedules \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} With 2PL, a transaction always locks the data item being read or written just before every operation \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} With 2PL, once a lock is released on any data item inside a transaction, no more locks on any data item can be acquired \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} A deadlock is possible with 2PL
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A;C;D} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Databases \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Transactions and concurrency control}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Two-Phase Locking (2PL) guarantees conflict serializability (A). In 2PL, once a lock is released (shrinking phase), no new locks can be acquired (C). Basic 2PL is susceptible to deadlocks due to circular wait conditions (D). Statement B describes conservative/static locking, not general 2PL.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[

    }
]
\textbf{Q.179} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS1\_CS\_Q15}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

A schedule of three database transactions $T_1, T_2$, and $T_3$ is shown. $R_i(A)$ and $W_i(A)$ denote read and write of data item $A$ by transaction $T_i$, $i= 1,2,3$. The transaction $T_1$ aborts at the end. Which other transaction(s) will be required to be rolled back?
\[ R\_1(X) \; W\_1(Y) \; R\_2(X) \; R\_2(Y) \; R\_3(Y) \; ABORT(T\_1) \]

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} Only $T_2$ & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} Only $T_3$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} Both $T_2$ and $T_3$ & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} Neither $T_2$ nor $T_3$
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large C} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Databases \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Transactions and concurrency control}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Transaction $T_1$ writes to item $Y$ ($W_1(Y)$). Subsequently, both $T_2$ and $T_3$ read uncommitted data item $Y$ ($R_2(Y)$ and $R_3(Y)$) written by $T_1$ before $T_1$ commits. When $T_1$ aborts, a cascading rollback is triggered, requiring both $T_2$ and $T_3$ to be rolled back.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

]
\textbf{Q.180} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS1\_CS\_Q21}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider a $B^+$ tree with 5 nodes, in which a node can store at most 3 key values. The value 23 is now inserted in the $B^+$ tree. Which of the following option(s) is/are CORRECT?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} None of the nodes will split. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} At least one node will split and redistribute. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} The total number of nodes will remain same. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} The height of the tree will increase.
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B;D} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Databases \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{File organization, indexing (e.g., B and B+ trees)}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small With maximum key capacity of 3 keys per node, the targeted leaf node containing keys $[20, 21, 22]$ is already full. Inserting 23 causes this leaf node to overflow and split, propagating a split upward to the already-saturated root, which creates a new root node and increases tree height.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.181} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS2\_CS\_Q27}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

An audit of a banking transactions system has found that on an earlier occasion, two joint holders of account $A$ attempted simultaneous transfers of Rs. 10000 each from account $A$ to account $B$. Both transactions read the same value, Rs. 11000, as the initial balance in $A$ and were allowed to go through. $B$ was credited Rs. 10000 twice. $A$ was debited only once and ended up with a balance of Rs. 1000. Which of the following properties is/are certain to have been violated by the system?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} Atomicity & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} Consistency \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} Isolation & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} Durability
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B;C} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Databases \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Transactions and concurrency control: ACID properties}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Both transactions read the same uncommitted state and overwrote each other (lost update anomaly), violating Isolation. The final balance violates the database invariant (starting 11000 debited $2 \times 10000$ cannot yield 1000), violating Consistency.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

\textbf{Q.182} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2018 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2018\_CS\_GA\_Q13}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Match the following:
\begin{center}
\begin{tabular}{ll@{\hspace{2cm}}ll}
\textbf{Field} & & \textbf{Length in bits} & \\
P. & UDP Header's Port Number & I. & 48 \\
Q. & Ethernet MAC Address & II. & 8 \\
R. & IPv6 Next Header & III. & 32 \\
S. & TCP Header's Sequence Number & IV. & 16
\end{tabular}
\end{center}

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} P-III, Q-I, R-II, S-IV & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} P-IV, Q-II, R-I, S-III \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} P-IV, Q-I, R-II, S-III & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} P-I, Q-IV, R-II, S-III
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large C} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Networks \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Concept of layering: OSI and TCP/IP Protocol Stacks}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small UDP port numbers are 16 bits long (P-IV). Ethernet MAC addresses are 48 bits long (Q-I). The IPv6 Next Header field is 8 bits long (R-II). TCP sequence numbers are 32 bits long (S-III). Hence the correct matching is P-IV, Q-I, R-II, S-III.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.183} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2018 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2018\_CS\_GA\_Q14}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider the following statements regarding the slow start phase of the TCP congestion control algorithm. Note that $cwnd$ stands for the congestion window size and $MSS$ denotes the Maximum Segment Size.
\begin{itemize}[leftmargin=*, nosep]
    \item[(i)] The $cwnd$ increases by 2 $MSS$ on every successful acknowledgement.
    \item[(ii)] The $cwnd$ increases by 1 $MSS$ on every successful acknowledgement.
    \item[(iii)] The $cwnd$ increases by 1 $MSS$ every round trip time.
    \item[(iv)] The $cwnd$ approximately doubles every round trip time.
\end{itemize}
Which of the following statements is/are CORRECT?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} (i) and (iii) only & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} (i) and (iv) only \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} (ii) and (iv) only & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} (ii) and (iii) only
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large C} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Networks \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Transport layer: flow control and congestion control, UDP, TCP, sockets}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small In TCP slow start, the congestion window (`cwnd`) increases by 1 MSS for each acknowledged segment. Consequently, when all segments in a flight are acknowledged, `cwnd` approximately doubles every round-trip time (RTT). Thus, statement (iv) is TRUE.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

\begin{tcolorbox}[

        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.184} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[NAT $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2018 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2018\_CS\_GA\_Q25}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider a 1 Gbps network with an MTU of 1500 bytes and a 32-bit sequence number space. The minimum time (in seconds, rounded to the nearest integer) after which the sequence numbers can wrap around is \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_.

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent\textbf{Your Answer:} \fbox{\rule{0pt}{1.3ex}\hspace{3cm}}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large 34 to 34} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Networks \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Transport layer: flow control and congestion control, UDP, TCP, sockets}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small A 32-bit sequence number space contains $2^{32} = 4{,}294{,}967{,}296$ bytes. At a transmission rate of $1\text{ Gbps} = \frac{10^9}{8} = 1.25 \times 10^8\text{ bytes/s}$, the wrap-around time is $\frac{2^{32}}{1.25 \times 10^8} = \frac{4294967296}{125000000} \approx 34.36$ seconds $\approx 34$ seconds.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.185} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2022 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2022\_CS\_CS\_Q22}}

Consider an enterprise network with two Ethernet segments, a web server and a firewall, connected via three routers. What is the number of subnets inside the enterprise network?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} 3 & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} 12 \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} 6 & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} 8
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large C} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Networks \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Concept of layering: OSI and TCP/IP Protocol Stacks}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small In the enterprise topology, subnets are formed by distinct broadcast domains separated by router interfaces: 2 Ethernet LAN segments (2 subnets), 1 web server subnet (1 subnet), 1 internal firewall segment (1 subnet), and 2 point-to-point links between the routers (2 subnets), yielding a total of 6 subnets.}

\end{tcolorbox}

Consider the resolution of the domain name `www.gate.org.in` by a DNS resolver. Assume that no resource records are cached anywhere across the DNS servers and that iterative query mechanism is used in the resolution. The number of DNS query-response pairs involved in completely resolving the domain name is \_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\_\. (Answer in integer)

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent\textbf{Your Answer:} \fbox{\rule{0pt}{1.3ex}\hspace{3cm}}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large 4 to 4} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Networks \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Application layer protocols: DNS, SMTP, HTTP, FTP, Email}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small In an un-cached iterative resolution for `www.gate.org.in`: the resolver queries Root DNS server (1), `.in` TLD server (2), `.org.in` SLD server (3), and `gate.org.in` Authoritative server (4). Each query yields a response, totaling 4 query-response pairs.}

\end{tcolorbox}

\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Suppose two hosts are connected by a point-to-point link and they are configured to use Stop-and-Wait protocol for reliable data transfer. Identify in which one of the following scenarios, the utilization of the link is the lowest.

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} Longer link length and lower transmission rate \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} Longer link length and higher transmission rate \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} Shorter link length and lower transmission rate \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} Shorter link length and higher transmission rate
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Networks \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Transport layer: flow control and congestion control, UDP, TCP, sockets}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Link utilization in Stop-and-Wait is $\eta = \frac{1}{1 + 2a}$, where $a = \frac{T_{\text{prop}}}{T_{\text{trans}}} = \frac{d/v}{L/B} = \frac{d \cdot B}{v \cdot L}$. Utilization $\eta$ is lowest when parameter $a$ is maximized, which occurs when the link length $d$ is longer (increasing propagation delay) and the transmission rate $B$ is higher (decreasing packet transmission time).}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[

    }
]
\textbf{Q.188} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2023 CS} \quad \tiny\texttt{2023\_CS\_CS\_Q25}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Which of the following statements is/are INCORRECT about the OSPF (Open Shortest Path First) routing protocol used in the Internet?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} OSPF implements Bellman-Ford algorithm to find shortest paths. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} OSPF uses Dijkstra's shortest path algorithm to implement least-cost path routing. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} OSPF is used as an inter-domain routing protocol. \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} OSPF implements hierarchical routing.
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A, C} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Networks \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Routing protocols: shortest path, flooding, distance vector and link state routing}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small OSPF is an intra-domain (Interior Gateway) routing protocol that uses Dijkstra's link-state algorithm (not Bellman-Ford, which is used by distance-vector protocols like RIP). Therefore, statement A (implements Bellman-Ford) and statement C (used as inter-domain) are INCORRECT, which are the required answers.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.189} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS1\_CS\_Q16}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

A user starts browsing a webpage hosted at a remote server. The browser opens a single TCP connection to fetch the entire webpage from the server. The webpage consists of a top-level index page with multiple embedded image objects. Assume that all caches (e.g., DNS cache, browser cache) are all initially empty. The following packets leave the user's computer in some order:
\begin{enumerate}[label=(\roman*),itemsep=0pt,topsep=2pt]
\item HTTP GET request for the index page
\item DNS request to resolve the web server's name to its IP address
\item HTTP GET request for an image object
\item TCP SYN to open a connection to the web server
\end{enumerate}
Which one of the following is the CORRECT chronological order (earliest in time to latest) of the packets leaving the computer?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} (iv), (ii), (iii), (i) & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} (ii), (iv), (iii), (i) \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} (ii), (iv), (i), (iii) & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} (iv), (ii), (i), (iii)
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large C} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Networks \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Concept of layering: OSI and TCP/IP Protocol Stacks}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Initially all caches are empty. The client must first send a DNS query (ii) to resolve the server hostname to an IP. Next, it establishes a TCP connection by sending TCP SYN (iv). Once connected, it sends an HTTP GET request for the index HTML page (i). After parsing the HTML and identifying embedded images, it sends HTTP GET requests for the images (iii).}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

\textbf{Q.190} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS1\_CS\_Q29}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

TCP client P successfully establishes a connection to TCP server Q. Let $N_P$ denote the sequence number in the SYN sent from P to Q. Let $N_Q$ denote the acknowledgement number in the SYN ACK from Q to P. Which of the following statements is/are CORRECT?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} The sequence number $N_P$ is chosen randomly by P \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} The sequence number $N_P$ is always 0 for a new connection \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} The acknowledgement number $N_Q$ is equal to $N_P$ \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} The acknowledgement number $N_Q$ is equal to $N_P + 1$
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A;D} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Networks \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Transport layer: flow control and congestion control, UDP, TCP, sockets}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small During TCP connection establishment, the initial sequence number $N_P$ is chosen pseudo-randomly to avoid cross-connection packet collisions and mitigate spoofing attacks (A is TRUE). Because a SYN packet consumes exactly 1 sequence number in the sequence space, server $Q$ acknowledges it with $N_Q = N_P + 1$ (D is TRUE).}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[
    blanker,
    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.191} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS1\_CS\_Q31}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Which of the following fields is/are modified in the IP header of a packet going out of a network address translation (NAT) device from an internal network to an external network?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} Source IP & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} Destination IP \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} Header Checksum & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} Total Length
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A;C} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Networks \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Network Address Translation (NAT)}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small When a packet leaves a private subnet through an outbound NAT gateway, the router rewrites the private Source IP address to its public external IP address (A is modified). Because the IP header fields have changed, the IPv4 Header Checksum must be recalculated and replaced (C is modified). Destination IP and Total Length are unmodified.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[
    blanker,
    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,
    middle=16pt,
    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.192} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS2\_CS\_Q23}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Node X has a TCP connection open to node Y. The packets from X to Y go through an intermediate IP router R. Ethernet switch S is the first switch on the network path between X and R. Consider a packet sent from X to Y over this connection.
Which of the following statements is/are TRUE about the destination addresses?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} The destination IP address is the IP address of R \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} The destination IP address is the IP address of Y \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} The destination MAC address is the MAC address of S \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} The destination MAC address is the MAC address of Y
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Networks \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Concept of layering: OSI and TCP/IP Protocol Stacks}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small In an end-to-end IP network, the destination IP address remains invariant throughout transit and is always the IP address of final host Y. The destination MAC address changes hop-by-hop and is set to the MAC address of the next-hop router R (not switch S, which operates transparently at Layer 2).}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[
    blanker,
    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,
    middle=16pt,
    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.193} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS2\_CS\_Q28}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Which of the following statements about IPv4 fragmentation is/are TRUE?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} The fragmentation of an IP datagram is performed only at the source of the datagram \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} The fragmentation of an IP datagram is performed at any IP router which finds that the size of the datagram is greater than the MTU of the outgoing link \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} The reassembly of fragments is performed only at the destination of the datagram \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} The reassembly of fragments is performed at all intermediate routers along the path from the source to destination
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B;C} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Networks \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Fragmentation and IP addressing, IPv4, CIDR notation}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small In IPv4, any intermediate router encountering a packet larger than the outgoing link MTU performs fragmentation (B). In contrast, fragment reassembly is performed strictly at the final destination host (C) to prevent re-fragmentation overhead and handle out-of-order routing.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[
    blanker,
    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,
    middle=16pt,
    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.194} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2024 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2024\_CS2\_CS\_Q32}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Which of the following fields of an IP header is/are always modified by any router before it forwards the IP packet?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} Source IP Address & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} Protocol \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} Time to Live (TTL) & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} Header Checksum
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large C;D} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Networks \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Fragmentation and IP addressing, IPv4, CIDR notation}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Whenever an IP packet is processed by an intermediate router, its Time to Live (TTL) field is decremented by at least 1. Because the header content changes, the router must recompute and update the Header Checksum field. Source and Protocol remain unchanged.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[

    sidebyside,

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.195} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS1\_CS\_Q16}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Identify the ONE CORRECT matching between the OSI layers and their corresponding functionalities as shown.
\par\medskip
\begin{tabular}{ll}
\textbf{OSI Layers} & \textbf{Functionalities} \\
(a) Network layer & (I) Packet routing \\
(b) Transport layer & (II) Framing and error handling \\
(c) Datalink layer & (III) Host to host communication
\end{tabular}

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} (a)-(I), (b)-(II), (c)-(III) & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} (a)-(I), (b)-(III), (c)-(II) \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} (a)-(II), (b)-(I), (c)-(III) & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} (a)-(III), (b)-(II), (c)-(I)
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Networks \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Concept of layering: OSI and TCP/IP Protocol Stacks}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small The Network layer is responsible for packet routing across networks ((a)-(I)). The Transport layer provides end-to-end communication services between hosts ((b)-(III)). The Data Link layer handles framing, flow control, and physical error handling ((c)-(II)).}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

    }
]
\textbf{Q.196} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MSQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ No Neg]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS1} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS1\_CS\_Q22}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider the 3-way handshaking protocol for TCP connection establishment. Let the three packets exchanged during the connection establishment be denoted as P1, P2, and P3, in order. Which of the following option(s) is/are TRUE with respect to TCP header flags that are set in the packets?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} P3: SYN = 1, ACK = 1 & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} P2: SYN = 1, ACK = 1 \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} P2: SYN = 0, ACK = 1 & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} P1: SYN = 1
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B;D} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Networks \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Transport layer: flow control and congestion control, UDP, TCP, sockets}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small In TCP 3-way handshaking: Packet 1 (Client to Server) sends a SYN segment with flag $[\text{SYN}=1, \text{ACK}=0]$; Packet 2 (Server to Client) responds with $[\text{SYN}=1, \text{ACK}=1]$; Packet 3 (Client to Server) completes setup with $[\text{SYN}=0, \text{ACK}=1]$. Hence, P1 has SYN = 1 and P2 has SYN = 1, ACK = 1.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[
    blanker,
    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,

            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.197} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS2\_CS\_Q16}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider the following statements:
(i) Address Resolution Protocol (ARP) provides a mapping from an IP address to the corresponding hardware (link-layer) address.
(ii) A single TCP segment from a sender S to a receiver R cannot carry both data from S to R and acknowledgement for a segment from R to S.
Which ONE of the following is CORRECT?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} Both (i) and (ii) are TRUE & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} (i) is TRUE and (ii) is FALSE \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} (i) is FALSE and (ii) is TRUE & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} Both (i) and (ii) are FALSE
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large B} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Networks \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Basics of IP support protocols: ARP and TCP headers}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small ARP resolves an IPv4 address to a physical MAC (link-layer) address, so (i) is true. A TCP segment supports piggybacking, where data and acknowledgement flags/numbers are sent in the same segment, so (ii) is false.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

    }
]
\textbf{Q.198} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS2\_CS\_Q17}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider the routing protocols given in List I and the names given in List II:
\par\medskip
\begin{center}\small
\begin{tabular}{ll}
\toprule
List I & List II \\
\midrule
(i) Distance vector routing & (a) Bellman-Ford \\
(ii) Link state routing & (b) Dijkstra \\
\bottomrule
\end{tabular}\end{center}
\par\medskip
For matching of items in List I with those in List II, which ONE of the following options is CORRECT?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} (i) – (a) and (ii) – (b) & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} (i) – (a) and (ii) – (a) \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} (i) – (b) and (ii) – (a) & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} (i) – (b) and (ii) – (b)
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large A} \hfill \textit{\small [Easy]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Networks \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Routing protocols: distance vector and link state}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small Distance Vector routing is based on the distributed Bellman-Ford algorithm, whereas Link State routing uses Dijkstra's shortest path algorithm.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}

\textbf{Q.199} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS2\_CS\_Q18}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

A machine receives an IPv4 datagram. The protocol field of the IPv4 header has the protocol number of a protocol X. Which ONE of the following is NOT a possible candidate for X?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.98\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} Internet Control Message Protocol (ICMP) \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(B)} Internet Group Management Protocol (IGMP) \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} Open Shortest Path First (OSPF) \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(D)} Routing Information Protocol (RIP)
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large D} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Networks \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Concept of layering: OSI and TCP/IP protocol stacks}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small The IPv4 Protocol field identifies the payload carried directly in the IP datagram: ICMP (protocol 1), IGMP (protocol 2), and OSPF (protocol 89). In contrast, RIP runs as an application-layer process encapsulated inside UDP (port 520).}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
\noindent
\begin{tcolorbox}[
    blanker,
    sidebyside,
    sidebyside align=top,
    lefthand width=0.55\textwidth,
    top=4pt, bottom=4pt, left=0pt, right=0pt,
    middle=16pt,
    underlay={
        \draw[dashed, black!80, line width=0.8pt, dash pattern=on 4pt off 3pt]
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.north west) --
            ([xshift=0.55\textwidth+8pt]frame.south west);
    }
]
\textbf{Q.200} \quad {\footnotesize\textsc{[MCQ $\cdot$ 1 Mark(s) $\cdot$ Neg: 0.33]}} \hfill {\footnotesize\textbf{GATE 2025 CS2} \quad \tiny\texttt{2025\_CS2\_CS\_Q23}}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{4pt}

Consider a network that uses Ethernet and IPv4. Assume that IPv4 headers do not use any options field. Each Ethernet frame can carry a maximum of 1500 bytes in its data field. A UDP segment is transmitted. The payload (data) in the UDP segment is 7488 bytes.
Which ONE of the following choices has the CORRECT total number of fragments transmitted and the size of the last fragment including IPv4 header?

\par\vspace{6pt}\noindent
\begin{tabular}{@{}p{0.48\linewidth}@{\hspace{0.04\linewidth}}p{0.48\linewidth}@{}}
$\square$\ \textbf{(A)} 5 fragments, 1488 bytes & $\square$\ \textbf{(B)} 6 fragments, 88 bytes \\
\addlinespace[3pt]
$\square$\ \textbf{(C)} 6 fragments, 108 bytes & $\square$\ \textbf{(D)} 6 fragments, 116 bytes
\end{tabular}

\tcblower

\textbf{Official Key:} \textbf{\large D} \hfill \textit{\small [Medium]}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Topic:} Computer Networks \\
\textbf{Subtopic:} \textit{Fragmentation and IP addressing}
\vspace{2pt}\hrule height 0.3pt\vspace{3pt}
\textbf{Guide / Hint:} \\
{\small UDP payload is 7488 bytes + 8 bytes UDP header = 7496 bytes IP payload. Max IP payload per Ethernet fragment is $\lfloor(1500 - 20)/8\rfloor \times 8 = 1480$ bytes. Fragment sizes are 5 fragments of 1480 bytes ($5 \times 1480 = 7400$), leaving $7496 - 7400 = 96$ bytes for the 6th fragment, so total size is $96 + 20 = 116$ bytes.}

\end{tcolorbox}
\vspace{6pt}\hrule height 0.35pt\vspace{6pt}
